Uh, yeah… I guess the main thing is my eating got really messed up. Like, I used to track meals pretty carefully—calories, macros, all that project-manager brain stuff, I had spreadsheets for everything at one point, which honestly sounds ridiculous saying out loud—but then I just stopped caring completely.

I wasn’t really eating proper meals for days, just coffee and protein bars, kind of telling myself it was efficient, like I was optimizing time or something. And then I noticed my clothes were hanging loose, but only after the fact. I don’t know, it just felt like things went off the rails before I really caught up with it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not like losing control while eating. It’s almost more like I lost control of the structure around eating, if that makes sense.

I came in because it went completely off the rails—I stopped caring about the food tracking thing I used to do, and then I’d go days without real meals. Just coffee, protein bars, whatever was easy. I kept thinking of it like a productivity thing, like when I streamline a project plan and cut out the extra steps, except… food isn’t really supposed to work like that, I guess.

And then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. But it wasn’t me eating a huge amount and feeling out of control. It was more like I wasn’t eating properly at all.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, not in the sense of eating too much and not being able to stop. It’s more like I stopped being in control of the whole routine around it.

I used to track my food pretty carefully, like it was part of my system for the day—kind of like how I keep project timelines organized, everything in its column, everything accounted for. And then I just… stopped caring about it. Then it turned into days where I wasn’t really eating meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t interrupt anything.

So the “how much” part got away from me, but downward, I guess. I didn’t even really register it until my clothes started hanging loose.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think it was about wanting to look a certain way. It wasn’t like I was aiming for a body type or trying to fit into smaller clothes or anything.

It was more that my eating just… stopped functioning like a normal routine. I came in because it went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to do pretty consistently, and then I’d skip actual meals for days. Coffee, protein bars, whatever was easiest. Kind of like when a project gets stripped down to the bare minimum tasks, except I guess I did that to eating.

And then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. So no, the appearance thing wasn’t the point. It just sort of happened while everything got disorganized.

Anxious about eating in general? I don’t know… not exactly. I wouldn’t say I sit there afraid of food or afraid something bad will happen if I eat.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if it was to avoid feeling something exactly. Maybe? I mean, I didn’t sit down and think, “If I don’t eat, I won’t feel whatever.” It wasn’t that organized.

It was more like everything got stripped down to the easiest possible version. I came in because my eating went completely off the rails—I stopped caring about tracking it, and I used to be pretty consistent with that. Then it was just days of not really having meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were there and didn’t require stopping.

Like, with work, if a system breaks, sometimes you keep using the broken workaround longer than you should because technically it still functions. That’s kind of what it felt like. Except then my clothes started hanging loose, and that’s when I realized, okay, this isn’t just a messy routine anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so… or at least it happened when I was overwhelmed enough that normal routines just sort of dropped out.

I came in because my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to be pretty consistent with, and then I’d go days without real meals. It was coffee, protein bars, whatever didn’t require stopping and making an actual decision.

It’s like when a project gets chaotic and you start cutting “nonessential” steps just to keep moving, except eating somehow got treated like one of those steps. Which sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because obviously it’s not nonessential.

And then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed the weight part. So… yeah, I guess stress or being overwhelmed was tied into it, but at the time it felt more like everything just got disorganized.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in that way, no. I don’t remember thinking, “If I eat normally, I’ll gain weight.” It wasn’t like I was trying to keep weight off.

It was more that my eating just kind of… collapsed. I stopped caring about tracking it, and I used to be pretty good about that. Then days would go by where I didn’t have real meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t interrupt anything.

And the weird thing is I didn’t even register it as weight at first. My clothes started hanging loose, and that’s when it hit me that something was off. But I wasn’t avoiding normal food because I was scared of gaining weight, at least not that I can remember.

Then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained back the weight you lost?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d be upset exactly. I mean, maybe if my clothes suddenly didn’t fit, that would be annoying, but that’s not really what this has been about.\n\nThe reason I came in is more that my eating went completely off the rails. It’s almost more like I lost control of the structure around eating, if that makes sense.\n\nI came in because it went completely off the rails—I stopped caring about the food tracking thing I used to do, and then I’d go days without real meals. Just coffee, protein bars, whatever was easy. I kept thinking of it like a productivity thing, like when I streamline a project plan and cut out the extra steps, except… food isn’t really supposed to work like that, I guess.\n\nAnd then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. Coffee, protein bars, whatever was easiest. Maybe… I don’t know if I’d call it “coping” exactly. It wasn’t like I had a plan, like, “I’m upset so I’m not going to eat.” It was more that everything got so messy in my head and schedule that eating properly just fell off the list.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think it was fear of gaining weight. That wasn’t really the thought in my head.

It was more like… eating just stopped being a thing I was managing. I’d been pretty consistent with tracking food before, like I had my little system, and then that just fell apart. I’d go days without proper meals and just sort of run on coffee and protein bars because they were there and easy.

Actually, it’s kind of embarrassing because in project work I’m always the person saying, “If you don’t track it, it drifts,” and then I did exactly that with my own eating. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… maybe partly, but not in a dramatic way like I was disgusted by food or anything. It was more like I just didn’t want to deal with the whole process of it.

I came in because my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking it, and I used to be pretty on top of that—like with work, I track milestones and blockers and all that, so it’s weird that I just let this basic thing disappear. Then I’d go days without actual meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t require me to stop or think.

And honestly, I didn’t even catch on right away. My clothes started hanging loose, and that was the thing that made me go, oh, something’s happening here. So yeah, I guess “not wanting to deal with food at all” is closer than worrying about weight.

And then I noticed my clothes were hanging loose, but only after the fact. I mean, I didn’t get on a scale right away, which is weird because I used to be kind of obsessive about tracking that stuff—food, calories, macros, like I had it all organized—but then I just… stopped caring.\n\nI was skipping actual meals for days, mostly coffee and protein bars, telling myself it was practical or efficient. Then my clothes started feeling loose, kind of hanging off me, and that’s when I realized I’d probably lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “afraid” is the word. I get like that with systems sometimes, like if it’s streamlined then it must be fine, but obviously… yeah. Then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained back the weight you lost?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d be upset exactly. I mean, maybe if my clothes suddenly didn’t fit, that would be annoying, but that’s not really what this has been about.\n\nThe reason I came in is more that my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to do all the time, and then I was just skipping real meals for days—coffee, protein bars, whatever was easy. It felt efficient, like I had simplified the whole thing, but then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. So gaining it back… I don’t know, I think I’d be more concerned about getting back to normal eating than the number itself.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever afraid of losing control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: No, not like losing control while eating. It’s almost more like I lost control of the structure around eating, if that makes sense.\n\nI came in because it went completely off the rails—I stopped caring about the food tracking thing I used to do, and then I’d go days without real meals. Just coffee, protein bars, whatever was easy. I kept thinking of it like a productivity thing, like when I streamline a project plan and cut out the extra steps, except… food isn’t really supposed to work like that, I guess.\n\nAnd then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. But it wasn’t me eating a huge amount and feeling out of control. It was more like I wasn’t eating properly at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're not in control of what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in the sense of eating too much and not being able to stop. It’s more like I stopped being in control of the whole routine around it.\n\nI used to track my food pretty carefully, like it was part of my system for the day—kind of like how I keep project timelines organized, everything in its column, everything accounted for. And then I just… stopped caring about it. Then it turned into days where I wasn’t really eating meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t interrupt anything.\n\nSo the “how much” part got away from me, but downward, I guess. I didn’t even really register it until my clothes started hanging loose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits changed because you wanted to look a certain way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was about wanting to look a certain way. It wasn’t like I was aiming for a body type or trying to fit into smaller clothes or anything.\n\nIt was more that my eating just… stopped functioning like a normal routine. I came in because it went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to do pretty consistently, and then I’d skip actual meals for days. Coffee, protein bars, whatever was easiest. Kind of like when a project gets stripped down to the bare minimum tasks, except I guess I did that to eating.\n\nAnd then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. So no, the appearance thing wasn’t the point. It just sort of happened while everything got disorganized.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever anxious about eating in general?\n\nPatient: Anxious about eating in general? I don’t know… not exactly. I wouldn’t say I sit there afraid of food or afraid something bad will happen if I eat.\n\nIt’s more like eating became this annoying interruption, like when you’re deep in a schedule or a deliverable and someone throws in a meeting that breaks your whole flow. So I’d put it off, and then suddenly it’s late and I’ve had coffee and maybe a bar and I’m telling myself that counts.\n\nBut anxiety, specifically… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as fear around eating itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you stopped taking care of your eating habits as a way to avoid feeling something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it was to avoid feeling something exactly. I came in because my eating went completely off the rails—I stopped caring about tracking it, and I used to be pretty consistent with that. Then it was just days of not really having meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were there and didn’t require stopping.\n\nLike, with work, if a system breaks, sometimes you keep using the broken workaround longer than you should because technically it still functions. That’s kind of what it felt like. Except then my clothes started hanging loose, and that’s when I realized, okay, this isn’t just a messy routine anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits changed when you were feeling stressed or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… or at least it happened when I was overwhelmed enough that normal routines just sort of dropped out.\n\nI came in because my eating went completely off the rails. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I know it sounds like that from the outside because I stopped eating proper meals and my clothes got loose, but it wasn’t like I was thinking, “Don’t gain weight.”

It was more that my eating just went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which is strange because tracking is basically my whole work brain—deliverables, timelines, whatever—and then with food I just… dropped it. I’d go days without real meals and just have coffee and protein bars because they were easy.

Honestly, I noticed the clothes thing before I really noticed the eating thing, if that makes sense.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I guess the main thing is my eating got really messed up. Like, I used to track meals pretty carefully—calories, macros, all that project-manager brain stuff, I had spreadsheets for everything at one point, which honestly sounds ridiculous saying out loud—but then I just stopped caring completely.\n\nI wasn’t really eating proper meals for days, just coffee and protein bars, kind of telling myself it was efficient, like I was optimizing time or something. And then I noticed my clothes were hanging loose, but only after the fact. I don’t know, it just felt like things went off the rails before I really caught up with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I didn’t get on a scale right away, which is weird because I used to be kind of obsessive about tracking that stuff—food, calories, macros, like I had it all organized—but then I just… stopped caring.\n\nI was skipping actual meals for days, mostly coffee and protein bars, telling myself it was practical or efficient. Then my clothes started feeling loose, kind of hanging off me, and that’s when I realized I’d probably lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “afraid” is the word. Like, I’m not looking in the mirror thinking I need to be smaller, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like… I got caught up in the logistics of not eating, if that makes sense. Which sounds stupid. I used to be very numbers-oriented with food, but lately it wasn’t really about weight gain so much as just not paying attention. Or telling myself I was being efficient. I don’t think I’m scared of gaining weight, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not that I can think of. It’s been more the opposite problem, honestly. I came in because my eating just kind of fell apart—I stopped doing the tracking thing I used to do, and then I wasn’t eating real meals for days.\n\nIt was like coffee, protein bars, whatever was easy, and I kept telling myself it was efficient. I get like that with systems sometimes, like if it’s streamlined then it must be fine, but obviously… yeah. Then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained back the weight you lost?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d be upset exactly. I mean, maybe if my clothes suddenly didn’t fit, that would be annoying, but that’s not really what this has been about.\n\nThe reason I came in is more that my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to do all the time, and then I was just skipping real meals for days—coffee, protein bars, whatever was easy. It felt efficient, like I had simplified the whole thing, but then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. So gaining it back… I don’t know, I think I’d be more concerned about getting back to normal eating than the number itself.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever afraid of losing control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: No, not like losing control while eating. It’s almost more like I lost control of the structure around eating, if that makes sense.\n\nI came in because it went completely off the rails—I stopped caring about the food tracking thing I used to do, and then I’d go days without real meals. Just coffee, protein bars, whatever was easy. I kept thinking of it like a productivity thing, like when I streamline a project plan and cut out the extra steps, except… food isn’t really supposed to work like that, I guess.\n\nAnd then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. But it wasn’t me eating a huge amount and feeling out of control. It was more like I wasn’t eating properly at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're not in control of what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in the sense of eating too much and not being able to stop. It’s more like I stopped being in control of the whole routine around it.\n\nI used to track my food pretty carefully, like it was part of my system for the day—kind of like how I keep project timelines organized, everything in its column, everything accounted for. And then I just… stopped caring about it. Then it turned into days where I wasn’t really eating meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t interrupt anything.\n\nSo the “how much” part got away from me, but downward, I guess. I didn’t even really register it until my clothes started hanging loose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits changed because you wanted to look a certain way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was about wanting to look a certain way. It wasn’t like I was aiming for a body type or trying to fit into smaller clothes or anything.\n\nIt was more that my eating just… stopped functioning like a normal routine. I came in because it went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to do pretty consistently, and then I’d skip actual meals for days. Coffee, protein bars, whatever was easiest. Kind of like when a project gets stripped down to the bare minimum tasks, except I guess I did that to eating.\n\nAnd then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. So no, the appearance thing wasn’t the point. It just sort of happened while everything got disorganized.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever anxious about eating in general?\n\nPatient: Anxious about eating in general? I don’t know… not exactly. I wouldn’t say I sit there afraid of food or afraid something bad will happen if I eat.\n\nIt’s more like eating became this annoying interruption, like when you’re deep in a schedule or a deliverable and someone throws in a meeting that breaks your whole flow. So I’d put it off, and then suddenly it’s late and I’ve had coffee and maybe a bar and I’m telling myself that counts.\n\nBut anxiety, specifically… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as fear around eating itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you stopped taking care of your eating habits as a way to avoid feeling something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it was to avoid feeling something exactly. Maybe? I mean, I didn’t sit down and think, “If I don’t eat, I won’t feel whatever.” It wasn’t that organized.\n\nIt was more like everything got stripped down to the easiest possible version. I came in because my eating went completely off the rails—I stopped caring about tracking it, and I used to be pretty consistent with that. Then it was just days of not really having meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were there and didn’t require stopping.\n\nLike, with work, if a system breaks, sometimes you keep using the broken workaround longer than you should because technically it still functions. That’s kind of what it felt like. Except then my clothes started hanging loose, and that’s when I realized, okay, this isn’t just a messy routine anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits changed when you were feeling stressed or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… or at least it happened when I was overwhelmed enough that normal routines just sort of dropped out.\n\nI came in because my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to be pretty consistent with, and then I’d go days without real meals. It was coffee, protein bars, whatever didn’t require stopping and making an actual decision.\n\nIt’s like when a project gets chaotic and you start cutting “nonessential” steps just to keep moving, except eating somehow got treated like one of those steps. Which sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because obviously it’s not nonessential.\n\nAnd then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed the weight part. So… yeah, I guess stress or being overwhelmed was tied into it, but at the time it felt more like everything just got disorganized.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that if you went back to eating normally, you would gain weight?\n\nPatient: Not really in that way, no. I don’t remember thinking, “If I eat normally, I’ll gain weight.” It wasn’t like I was trying to keep weight off.\n\nIt was more that my eating just kind of… collapsed. I stopped caring about tracking it, and I used to be pretty good about that. Then days would go by where I didn’t have real meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t interrupt anything.\n\nAnd the weird thing is I didn’t even register it as weight at first. My clothes started hanging loose, and that’s when it hit me that something was off. But I wasn’t avoiding normal food because I was scared of gaining weight, at least not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Did you stop eating properly as a way to cope with difficult emotions?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I don’t know if I’d call it “coping” exactly. It wasn’t like I had a plan, like, “I’m upset so I’m not going to eat.” It was more that everything got so messy in my head and schedule that eating properly just fell off the list.\n\nI came in because my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to be pretty consistent about, and then I’d skip real meals for days. It was just coffee and protein bars, because they were easy and I didn’t have to stop what I was doing.\n\nActually, I remember thinking it was kind of like when a work dashboard is broken but you keep using it because rebuilding it would take too much time—except the dashboard was, you know, my meals. And then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed what was happening.\n\nSo… maybe difficult emotions were part of it, but at the time it felt more like I just stopped maintaining that whole system.\n\nDoctor: Did your loss of interest in eating have anything to do with a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was fear of gaining weight. That wasn’t really the thought in my head.\n\nIt was more like… eating just stopped being a thing I was managing. I’d been pretty consistent with tracking food before, like I had my little system, and then that just fell apart. I’d go days without proper meals and just sort of run on coffee and protein bars because they were there and easy.\n\nActually, it’s kind of embarrassing because in project work I’m always the person saying, “If you don’t track it, it drifts,” and then I did exactly that with my own eating. My clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed what was happening. But no, I wasn’t avoiding food because I was scared of gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: Did your loss of interest in eating have anything to do with not wanting to deal with food at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe partly, but not in a dramatic way like I was disgusted by food or anything. It was more like I just didn’t want to deal with the whole process of it.\n\nI came in because my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking it, and I used to be pretty on top of that—like with work, I track milestones and blockers and all that, so it’s weird that I just let this basic thing disappear. Then I’d go days without actual meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t require me to stop or think.\n\nAnd honestly, I didn’t even catch on right away. My clothes started hanging loose, and that was the thing that made me go, oh, something’s happening here. So yeah, I guess “not wanting to deal with food at all” is closer than worrying about weight.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were trying to avoid gaining weight when you stopped tracking and eating regular meals?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was about trying not to gain weight. It was more like the whole eating system just… disappeared on me.\n\nI came in because my eating went completely off the rails. I don’t know if I’d call it emotionally uncomfortable exactly. It wasn’t like I’d look at food and feel some big wave of dread or shame or anything.

It was more like… the whole thing just fell out of my routine. But with food, I just stopped tracking it, stopped caring. Then I’d realize I’d gone days without real meals and was basically running on coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t make me stop.

And the strange part is I noticed my clothes hanging loose before I really noticed how far off my eating had gotten. So, uncomfortable? Maybe in the sense that dealing with meals felt like too much, but not emotional in the way I think you mean.

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It wasn’t about avoiding weight gain, no. It was another reason—well, not even a clean reason, really. My eating just kind of fell apart.

I stopped caring about tracking food, which is odd because I track everything else. I mean, at work I’m the guy with the color-coded timelines and the annoying follow-up notes, so you’d think food would be the same, but it just vanished from the system. Then I’d go days without actual meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and I didn’t have to think about them.

And honestly, I noticed my clothes hanging loose before I really registered how much I wasn’t eating. So no, it wasn’t me trying to keep weight off. It was more like I stopped functioning normally around meals.

---------- turn 19 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I guess the main thing is my eating got really messed up. Like, I used to track meals pretty carefully—calories, macros, all that project-manager brain stuff, I had spreadsheets for everything at one point, which honestly sounds ridiculous saying out loud—but then I just stopped caring completely.\n\nI wasn’t really eating proper meals for days, just coffee and protein bars, kind of telling myself it was efficient, like I was optimizing time or something. And then I noticed my clothes were hanging loose, but only after the fact. I don’t know, it just felt like things went off the rails before I really caught up with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I didn’t get on a scale right away, which is weird because I used to be kind of obsessive about tracking that stuff—food, calories, macros, like I had it all organized—but then I just… stopped caring.\n\nI was skipping actual meals for days, mostly coffee and protein bars, telling myself it was practical or efficient. Then my clothes started feeling loose, kind of hanging off me, and that’s when I realized I’d probably lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “afraid” is the word. Like, I’m not looking in the mirror thinking I need to be smaller, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like… I got caught up in the logistics of not eating, if that makes sense. Which sounds stupid. I used to be very numbers-oriented with food, but lately it wasn’t really about weight gain so much as just not paying attention. Or telling myself I was being efficient. I don’t think I’m scared of gaining weight, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes where you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not that I can think of. It’s been more the opposite problem, honestly. I came in because my eating just kind of fell apart—I stopped doing the tracking thing I used to do, and then I wasn’t eating real meals for days.\n\nIt was like coffee, protein bars, whatever was easy, and I kept telling myself it was efficient. I get like that with systems sometimes, like if it’s streamlined then it must be fine, but obviously… yeah. Then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained back the weight you lost?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d be upset exactly. I mean, maybe if my clothes suddenly didn’t fit, that would be annoying, but that’s not really what this has been about.\n\nThe reason I came in is more that my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to do all the time, and then I was just skipping real meals for days—coffee, protein bars, whatever was easy. It felt efficient, like I had simplified the whole thing, but then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. So gaining it back… I don’t know, I think I’d be more concerned about getting back to normal eating than the number itself.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever afraid of losing control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: No, not like losing control while eating. It’s almost more like I lost control of the structure around eating, if that makes sense.\n\nI came in because it went completely off the rails—I stopped caring about the food tracking thing I used to do, and then I’d go days without real meals. Just coffee, protein bars, whatever was easy. I kept thinking of it like a productivity thing, like when I streamline a project plan and cut out the extra steps, except… food isn’t really supposed to work like that, I guess.\n\nAnd then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. But it wasn’t me eating a huge amount and feeling out of control. It was more like I wasn’t eating properly at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're not in control of what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in the sense of eating too much and not being able to stop. It’s more like I stopped being in control of the whole routine around it.\n\nI used to track my food pretty carefully, like it was part of my system for the day—kind of like how I keep project timelines organized, everything in its column, everything accounted for. And then I just… stopped caring about it. Then it turned into days where I wasn’t really eating meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t interrupt anything.\n\nSo the “how much” part got away from me, but downward, I guess. I didn’t even really register it until my clothes started hanging loose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits changed because you wanted to look a certain way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was about wanting to look a certain way. It wasn’t like I was aiming for a body type or trying to fit into smaller clothes or anything.\n\nIt was more that my eating just… stopped functioning like a normal routine. I came in because it went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to do pretty consistently, and then I’d skip actual meals for days. Coffee, protein bars, whatever was easiest. Kind of like when a project gets stripped down to the bare minimum tasks, except I guess I did that to eating.\n\nAnd then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed I’d lost weight. So no, the appearance thing wasn’t the point. It just sort of happened while everything got disorganized.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever anxious about eating in general?\n\nPatient: Anxious about eating in general? I don’t know… not exactly. I wouldn’t say I sit there afraid of food or afraid something bad will happen if I eat.\n\nIt’s more like eating became this annoying interruption, like when you’re deep in a schedule or a deliverable and someone throws in a meeting that breaks your whole flow. So I’d put it off, and then suddenly it’s late and I’ve had coffee and maybe a bar and I’m telling myself that counts.\n\nBut anxiety, specifically… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as fear around eating itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you stopped taking care of your eating habits as a way to avoid feeling something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it was to avoid feeling something exactly. Maybe? I mean, I didn’t sit down and think, “If I don’t eat, I won’t feel whatever.” It wasn’t that organized.\n\nIt was more like everything got stripped down to the easiest possible version. I came in because my eating went completely off the rails—I stopped caring about tracking it, and I used to be pretty consistent with that. Then it was just days of not really having meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were there and didn’t require stopping.\n\nLike, with work, if a system breaks, sometimes you keep using the broken workaround longer than you should because technically it still functions. That’s kind of what it felt like. Except then my clothes started hanging loose, and that’s when I realized, okay, this isn’t just a messy routine anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits changed when you were feeling stressed or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… or at least it happened when I was overwhelmed enough that normal routines just sort of dropped out.\n\nI came in because my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to be pretty consistent with, and then I’d go days without real meals. It was coffee, protein bars, whatever didn’t require stopping and making an actual decision.\n\nIt’s like when a project gets chaotic and you start cutting “nonessential” steps just to keep moving, except eating somehow got treated like one of those steps. Which sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because obviously it’s not nonessential.\n\nAnd then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed the weight part. So… yeah, I guess stress or being overwhelmed was tied into it, but at the time it felt more like everything just got disorganized.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that if you went back to eating normally, you would gain weight?\n\nPatient: Not really in that way, no. I don’t remember thinking, “If I eat normally, I’ll gain weight.” It wasn’t like I was trying to keep weight off.\n\nIt was more that my eating just kind of… collapsed. I stopped caring about tracking it, and I used to be pretty good about that. Then days would go by where I didn’t have real meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t interrupt anything.\n\nAnd the weird thing is I didn’t even register it as weight at first. My clothes started hanging loose, and that’s when it hit me that something was off. But I wasn’t avoiding normal food because I was scared of gaining weight, at least not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Did you stop eating properly as a way to cope with difficult emotions?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I don’t know if I’d call it “coping” exactly. It wasn’t like I had a plan, like, “I’m upset so I’m not going to eat.” It was more that everything got so messy in my head and schedule that eating properly just fell off the list.\n\nI came in because my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which I used to be pretty consistent about, and then I’d skip real meals for days. It was just coffee and protein bars, because they were easy and I didn’t have to stop what I was doing.\n\nActually, I remember thinking it was kind of like when a work dashboard is broken but you keep using it because rebuilding it would take too much time—except the dashboard was, you know, my meals. And then my clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed what was happening.\n\nSo… maybe difficult emotions were part of it, but at the time it felt more like I just stopped maintaining that whole system.\n\nDoctor: Did your loss of interest in eating have anything to do with a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was fear of gaining weight. That wasn’t really the thought in my head.\n\nIt was more like… eating just stopped being a thing I was managing. I’d been pretty consistent with tracking food before, like I had my little system, and then that just fell apart. I’d go days without proper meals and just sort of run on coffee and protein bars because they were there and easy.\n\nActually, it’s kind of embarrassing because in project work I’m always the person saying, “If you don’t track it, it drifts,” and then I did exactly that with my own eating. My clothes started hanging loose before I even really noticed what was happening. But no, I wasn’t avoiding food because I was scared of gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: Did your loss of interest in eating have anything to do with not wanting to deal with food at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe partly, but not in a dramatic way like I was disgusted by food or anything. It was more like I just didn’t want to deal with the whole process of it.\n\nI came in because my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking it, and I used to be pretty on top of that—like with work, I track milestones and blockers and all that, so it’s weird that I just let this basic thing disappear. Then I’d go days without actual meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t require me to stop or think.\n\nAnd honestly, I didn’t even catch on right away. My clothes started hanging loose, and that was the thing that made me go, oh, something’s happening here. So yeah, I guess “not wanting to deal with food at all” is closer than worrying about weight.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were trying to avoid gaining weight when you stopped tracking and eating regular meals?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was about trying not to gain weight. It was more like the whole eating system just… disappeared on me.\n\nI came in because my eating went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking my food, which is weird because I’m usually very tracking-oriented—work stuff, tasks, deadlines, all of that. But with food, I just stopped. Then I’d skip real meals for days and run on coffee and protein bars because they were simple and right there.\n\nAnd I didn’t even really clock it at first. My clothes started hanging loose, and that was what made me realize, okay, this isn’t just me being busy or disorganized. But no, I wasn’t sitting there thinking I needed to avoid gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like not eating was a way to prevent yourself from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know it sounds like that from the outside because I stopped eating proper meals and my clothes got loose, but it wasn’t like I was thinking, “Don’t gain weight.”\n\nIt was more that my eating just went completely off the rails. I stopped caring about tracking food, which is strange because tracking is basically my whole work brain—deliverables, timelines, whatever—and then with food I just… dropped it. I’d go days without real meals and just have coffee and protein bars because they were easy.\n\nHonestly, I noticed the clothes thing before I really noticed the eating thing, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you avoid eating because it felt emotionally uncomfortable for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it emotionally uncomfortable exactly. It wasn’t like I’d look at food and feel some big wave of dread or shame or anything.\n\nIt was more like… the whole thing just fell out of my routine. Which is weird because I’m usually very structured—like at work I’m the person with the spreadsheet and the milestones and the little status notes nobody asked for. But with food, I just stopped tracking it, stopped caring. Then I’d realize I’d gone days without real meals and was basically running on coffee and protein bars because they were easy and didn’t make me stop.\n\nAnd the strange part is I noticed my clothes hanging loose before I really noticed how far off my eating had gotten. So, uncomfortable? Maybe in the sense that dealing with meals felt like too much, but not emotional in the way I think you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did your eating habits change because you were trying to avoid gaining weight or for another reason?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t about avoiding weight gain, no. It was another reason—well, not even a clean reason, really. My eating just kind of fell apart.\n\nI stopped caring about tracking food, which is odd because I track everything else. I mean, at work I’m the guy with the color-coded timelines and the annoying follow-up notes, so you’d think food would be the same, but it just vanished from the system. Then I’d go days without actual meals, just coffee and protein bars because they were easy and I didn’t have to think about them.\n\nAnd honestly, I noticed my clothes hanging loose before I really registered how much I wasn’t eating. So no, it wasn’t me trying to keep weight off. It was more like I stopped functioning normally around meals.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
